## INTRODUCTION

Biometrics is a rapidly evolving field that deals with the identification and authentication of individuals based on their unique biological or behavioural characteristics. These characteristics, also known as biometric identifiers, have been used for centuries to establish identity.

Early examples include pharaohs being identified by their mummy masks and signatures serving as a form of identification \[[1](#9789815322316-C6.htm#r1)\]. The term “biometrics” itself is derived from the Greek words “bios” (life) and “metrein” (to measure). The scientific study of biometrics began in the late 19th century with the work of Alphonse Bertillon, who developed a system of anthropometry (body measurements) for criminal identification \[[2](#9789815322316-C6.htm#r2)\].

**Categories of Biometric Identifiers:** Biometric identifiers can be broadly classified into two main categories: physiological and behavioural.

**Physiological Characteristics:** These are unique physical attributes of a person that remain relatively constant throughout life. Examples include:

 

*   ***Fingerprints:*** The intricate patterns of ridges and valleys on the fingertips.
*   ***Facial Recognition:*** The geometric configuration of facial features like eyes, nose, and mouth.
*   ***Iris Recognition:*** The unique patterns in the coloured ring of the eye.
*   ***Retinal Scans:*** The intricate blood vessel patterns on the retina at the back of the eye.

**Behavioural Characteristics:** These are unique patterns of behaviour that can be used to identify individuals. Examples include:

 

*   ***Signature Dynamics:*** The way a person signs their name, including pressure, speed, and penmanship variations.
*   ***Gait Recognition:*** The unique way a person walks or runs \[[3](#9789815322316-C6.htm#r3)\].
*   **Voice Recognition:** The unique characteristics of a person's voice, including pitch, tone, and cadence \[[4](#9789815322316-C6.htm#r4)\].
*   ***Keystroke dynamics:*** The unique typing rhythm and pattern in which a user types \[[5](#9789815322316-C6.htm#r5)\].

**Applications of Biometrics and Security Benefits:** Biometric systems offer a significant advantage over traditional authentication methods like passwords or tokens, which can be lost, stolen, or shared. Biometric identifiers are inherent to an individual and are very difficult to forge. As a result, biometrics are finding increasing applications in various security-sensitive areas, including \[[6](#9789815322316-C6.htm#r6)\]:

 

*   ***Border Control and Immigration:*** Biometrics are used to verify the identity of travellers and prevent unauthorized entry.
*   ***Access Control:*** Biometric systems are used to control access to physical locations or digital resources.
*   ***Financial Transactions:*** Biometrics can be used to verify identity for online banking or secure payments.
*   ***Law Enforcement:*** Biometric databases can be used to identify criminals or missing persons.

The use of biometrics has significantly enhanced security measures by providing a more reliable and tamper-proof method of identification and authentication.

## TRADITIONAL BIOMETRIC SYSTEMS AND THEIR LIMITATIONS

The typical workflow involves a two-process procedure \[[7](#9789815322316-C6.htm#r7)\], enrolment and authentication/identification. During the enrolment stage, a user's biometric data is captured, features are extracted, a template is generated and stored securely in a database. This data can be a fingerprint image, a facial scan, an iris pattern, or a voice recording, depending on the chosen modality. During the identification stage, the system gets a new biometric sample and checks who it belongs to. A special program analyzes the sample and decides if it matches someone already in the system (a real user) or not (an impostor). This decision is made by comparing the new sample to the information stored for each user. Despite their widespread adoption, traditional biometric systems face limitations that hinder their effectiveness. Some of the common issues faced by the biometric system are:

 

*   ***Vulnerability to Spoofing Attacks:*** A significant concern is the potential for criminals to bypass these systems using fake biometric replicas, such as fingerprint molds, high-resolution facial masks, or synthetically generated voice recordings \[[8](#9789815322316-C6.htm#r8)\]. Advancements in deepfake technology and readily available materials for creating spoofs pose a continuous challenge for traditional systems.
*   ***Accuracy Concerns:*** Several factors can impact the accuracy of traditional biometric systems, such as low-quality sensors, which can lead to noisy data and inaccurate feature extraction, ultimately affecting the matching process.
*   ***Environmental Factors*:** Variations in lighting, background noise, or changes in a person's appearance (*e.g*., wearing glasses, aging) can influence recognition accuracy \[[9](#9789815322316-C6.htm#r9)\].
*   ***Non-universality and Non-uniqueness:*** A small percentage of the population may have biometric features that are difficult to capture or lack sufficient distinctiveness for reliable identification (*e.g*., fingerprints with limited ridge patterns).
*   ***Privacy Issues:*** The storage and use of biometric data raise significant privacy concerns. Biometric data, unlike passwords, cannot be easily changed if compromised. Leaks or breaches of biometric databases can have severe consequences for user privacy and potentially lead to identity theft \[[10](#9789815322316-C6.htm#r10)\].
*   ***Limited Adaptability:*** Traditional systems may struggle to adapt to changes in user behaviour over time. For example, a fingerprint recognition system might not recognize a user with a scar or injury on their finger, or a voice recognition system might struggle with changes in voice due to illness \[[11](#9789815322316-C6.htm#r11)\].

These limitations highlight the need for innovative approaches to enhance the robustness, security, and adaptability of biometric systems. RL emerges as a promising technique to address these challenges and unlock new possibilities in biometrics.

**RL:** Imagine training a dog with rewards and punishments. That is the core idea behind RL. An RL agent interacts with an environment, takes actions, and receives rewards or penalties based on the outcome. Over time, the agent learns through trial and error to take actions that maximize its rewards \[[12](#9789815322316-C6.htm#r12)\]. Here's a breakdown of the key components in RL:

 

*   ***Agent:*** This represents the biometric system itself.
*   ***Environment:*** This encompasses the user, sensor data, and external factors like lighting.
*   ***Actions:*** These are decisions the system makes, such as adjusting thresholds or adapting feature extraction techniques.
*   ***Rewards:*** These are positive outcomes, like successful user identification or liveness detection.
*   ***Penalties:*** These are negative outcomes, like failing to identify a legitimate user or being fooled by a spoofing attempt.

### How can RL improve Biometrics?

By using RL, there are multiple advantages for improving biometric systems. Some of the most prominent of these are \[[13](#9789815322316-C6.htm#r13)^(\-)[15](#9789815322316-C6.htm#r15)\]:

 

*   ***Enhanced Liveness Detection:*** By constantly learning from user interaction data and receiving rewards for correctly identifying genuine users, RL algorithms can become adept at differentiating real biometrics from spoofing attempts. This can significantly improve the security of biometric systems.
*   ***Dynamic Multimodal Authentication:*** Biometric systems often combine multiple modalities (*e.g*., fingerprint and facial recognition) for improved security. RL can empower these systems to dynamically adjust the weight given to each modality based on factors like sensor quality or user behaviour. This ensures optimal performance under varying conditions.
*   ***Continuous User Verification:*** Current systems typically rely on a one-time authentication. RL can enable continuous verification throughout a user session. The system can learn user behaviour patterns and continuously assess if the user remains legitimate. This can significantly improve security and prevent unauthorized access during extended sessions.
*   ***Adaptive System Optimization:*** Biometric systems can benefit from real-time adjustments based on environmental conditions (*e.g*., lighting variations) or user behaviour changes. RL can be employed to learn and adapt system parameters (*e.g*., image pre-processing techniques, feature extraction algorithms) in real-time, ensuring optimal performance under various conditions.

By incorporating RL, biometric systems can become more robust, adaptable, and secure, addressing several challenges faced by traditional approaches. In the next section, we will delve deeper into the specific applications of RL in biometrics and explore the potential benefits in more detail.

## APPLICATIONS OF RL IN BIOMETRIC SYSTEMS

**Liveness Detection with RL in Biometrics:** Liveness detection is a critical component of biometric systems, ensuring that the presented biometric data originates from a living person and not a spoof (*e.g*., fingerprint mold, facial mask). Traditional approaches often rely on pre-defined thresholds or rules, which can be susceptible to evolving spoofing techniques. Here RL emerges as a promising approach for enhancing liveness detection.

### How does RL Excel in Liveness Detection?

There are multiple ways in which RL can help improve the accuracy of a biometric system. The first is continuous learning. Unlike static rule-based systems, RL algorithms can continuously learn from interaction data. As new spoofing techniques emerge, the RL agent constantly adapts its detection strategy, improving its ability to differentiate between genuine users and spoofing attempts. They implemented an RL-based approach for fingerprint liveness detection. The system achieved high accuracy even against sophisticated spoofing attempts like artificial fingerprints created using 3D printing technology. This highlights the advantage of RL in adapting to evolving threats. Another area includes adapting to diverse attacks. Spoofing attacks can vary significantly depending on the targeted modality (fingerprint, face, voice). RL's ability to learn from diverse data enables the agent to become adept at identifying a wider range of spoof types, enhancing the system's robustness. Building on this concept, a recent work \[[16](#9789815322316-C6.htm#r16)\] implemented an RL agent specifically designed to combat deepfake spoofing attacks in facial recognition. The agent learned to detect subtle inconsistencies in facial expressions and movements often present in deepfakes, achieving superior spoof detection compared to traditional methods. This demonstrates the effectiveness of RL in addressing novel spoofing techniques. The last area of impact is real-time feature extraction. RL can be integrated with deep learning models for feature extraction. The agent can learn to extract the most discriminative features in real-time, dynamically adjusting based on user interaction and potential spoofing attempts. In a recent study \[[17](#9789815322316-C6.htm#r17)\], the authors combined RL with a Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) for liveness detection in iris recognition. The RL agent learned to prioritize features that captured subtle physiological changes in the iris, such as blood vessel flow patterns, which are difficult to replicate in spoofs. This approach achieved significant improvements in liveness detection accuracy compared to CNN-based methods alone.

**Suitable RL Algorithms for Liveness Detection:** There are various algorithms that can be used for liveness detection. Some of the prominent ones are:

 

*   ***Deep Q-Networks (DQNs):*** This is a popular RL technique that has been successfully applied for liveness detection in fingerprint recognition \[[18](#9789815322316-C6.htm#r18)\]. A DQN agent interacts with the biometric data (environment) and receives rewards for correctly identifying live users and penalties for failing to detect spoofs. The agent continuously learns through trial and error, refining its decision-making for liveness detection.
*   ***Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO):*** This approach addresses limitations of DQN algorithms, particularly the risk of getting stuck in sub-optimal states. PPO ensures stable policy updates during the learning process, leading to more robust liveness detection capabilities.

A recent study \[[19](#9789815322316-C6.htm#r19)\] implemented a DQN-based RL approach for liveness detection in facial recognition. The system was trained on a dataset containing real facial videos and various types of spoof attempts (*e.g*., printed photos, videos). The RL agent learned to extract discriminative features, such as eye blinking patterns and subtle facial movements, effectively differentiating between genuine users and spoof attempts. This demonstrates the potential of RL for real-world liveness detection applications.

## CHALLENGES AND FUTURE DIRECTIONS

While RL offers exciting possibilities for biometric applications, some key challenges need to be addressed:

 

*   ***Privacy Concerns:*** Balancing robust user verification with data privacy is paramount. Techniques for anonymized data collection, federated learning, and differential privacy are crucial for ensuring user trust.
*   ***Explainability and Interpretability:*** Understanding the rationale behind RL decisions for user verification or system optimization is vital for security audits and user trust. Research on interpretable RL models that provide insights into the decision-making process is ongoing.
*   ***Adversarial Attacks:*** Malicious actors might attempt to manipulate their behaviour or the system itself to deceive RL-based verification systems. Techniques for improving the robustness of RL models against such attacks are crucial. This might involve adversarial training methods where the RL agent is exposed to simulated spoofing attempts during training.
*   ***Large Training Data Requirements:*** Effective RL for biometrics often requires a significant amount of labelled data, encompassing both genuine user interactions and diverse spoofing attempts. Collecting and labelling such data can be expensive and time-consuming. Techniques for synthetic data generation and transfer learning from similar domains can help mitigate this challenge.

Looking ahead, RL's future in biometrics involves seamless integration with emerging biometric modalities, user-centric and explainable RL for transparency and trust, and context-aware security for adaptive verification based on factors like time and location. Hybrid approaches combining RL with other machine learning techniques, as well as the development of security and privacy-preserving RL algorithms, hold further promise for the future of secure and user-friendly biometric authentication.

**Dynamic Multimodal Authentication:** Traditional biometric systems often rely on a single modality (*e.g*., fingerprint, facial recognition) for user authentication. However, this approach can be vulnerable to spoofing attacks that mimic a single biometric characteristic. Dynamic multimodal authentication, which combines multiple modalities, offers enhanced security. Here, RL emerges as a powerful tool to dynamically adjust the weight given to each modality based on factors like sensor quality or user behaviour.

### How RL Excels in Dynamic Multimodal Authentication

RL can help improve multimodal authentication using biometrics in multiple ways. The first being adaptive weighting of modalities. Unlike static systems that assign fixed weights to each modality, RL allows the system to learn optimal weights in real-time. This ensures that modalities with higher reliability (*e.g*., high-quality fingerprint sensor) contribute more to the authentication decision. The second method is by addressing sensor degradation. Sensor performance can degrade over time, impacting the reliability of captured biometric data. RL can learn to adjust reliance on specific modalities based on sensor health, ensuring robust authentication even with aging sensors \[[20](#9789815322316-C6.htm#r20)\]. Lastly, RL can also improve user behaviour context awareness. User behaviour patterns can provide valuable insights for authentication. RL can learn to consider factors like user location, time of day, and typical login attempts (*e.g*., sitting at home *vs.* using a public kiosk) when assessing the weight of each modality.

### Benefits of RL for Dynamic Multimodal Authentication

There are multiple advantages of RL in multimodal authentication, such as improved security, enhanced adaptability, and reduced user friction. By dynamically adjusting weights based on various factors, RL systems can achieve higher authentication accuracy and become more robust against spoofing attempts that target specific modalities. The system can adapt to changing user behaviour patterns, sensor performance, and evolving spoofing techniques, ensuring long-term security. In scenarios where a modality becomes unreliable (*e.g*., a dirty fingerprint sensor), the system can compensate by placing greater emphasis on alternative modalities, minimizing the need for re-authentication attempts. Imagine a system combining fingerprint scanning on a smartphone and facial recognition on a smartwatch for user authentication. During login, the RL agent analyzes factors like:

 

*   ***Fingerprint Sensor Quality:*** If the phone's fingerprint sensor is dusty or slightly damaged, the RL agent might decrease its weight in the authentication decision.
*   ***User Location:*** If the user is logging in from an unfamiliar location, the RL agent might increase the weight of facial recognition for added security.
*   ***Time of Day:*** For high-security transactions at night, the RL agent might require both fingerprint and facial recognition for authentication.

By dynamically adjusting weights based on these factors, the system achieves a more secure and adaptive authentication process. Although the use of RL can help improve system performance but there are a few challenges, such as:

 

*   ***Data Collection and Privacy:*** Training RL models for multimodal authentication requires data from various modalities, raising privacy concerns. Techniques for anonymized data collection and federated learning are crucial.
*   ***Computational Complexity:*** Real-time processing of multimodal data and weight adjustment with RL can be computationally expensive. Optimizing RL algorithms for efficient execution on mobile devices is an ongoing area of research.

Dynamic multimodal authentication with RL offers a promising approach for enhancing security and user experience in biometric systems. Addressing data privacy concerns, optimizing computational efficiency, and exploring integration with emerging biometric modalities are key areas for future exploration.

### Continuous User Verification with Reinforcement Learning: Enhancing Security and Privacy

Traditional biometric systems often rely on a one-time authentication process during login. This approach has limitations, as unauthorized users could gain access with stolen credentials and remain undetected for extended periods. RL offers a promising solution for enabling continuous user verification, which continuously monitors user behaviour throughout a session to assess legitimacy.

### How RL Achieves Continuous User Verification?

An RL agent is trained on user interaction data. This data can include typing patterns, mouse movements, touch gestures (on mobile devices), and even gaze patterns. Over time, the agent learns the unique behavioural characteristics of each user. For instance, a study \[[21](#9789815322316-C6.htm#r21)\] implemented an RL-based system for continuous authentication on smartphones. The system collected user data on typing patterns, swipe gestures, and app usage. The RL agent learned to differentiate between legitimate users and imposters based on these behavioural features.

For real-time anomaly detection during a user session, the RL agent continuously monitors user behaviour and compares it to the learned baseline. Significant deviations from the expected behaviour might indicate a potential security breach (*e.g*., someone else using the authorized user's account) \[[22](#9789815322316-C6.htm#r22)\]. A recent study \[[23](#9789815322316-C6.htm#r23)\] explored this concept for online banking transactions. The RL agent monitored user behaviour patterns such as login location, transaction time, and interaction patterns with the banking app. Deviations from the user's baseline triggered additional authentication steps to prevent unauthorized access.

For adaptive security measures based on the level of anomaly detected, the RL agent can trigger appropriate security measures. This might involve prompting for re-authentication, locking the session, or even alerting security personnel.

### Benefits of RL for Continuous Verification

 

*   ***Enhanced Security:*** By continuously monitoring user behaviour, RL systems can detect unauthorized access attempts much faster than traditional one-time authentication methods, improving overall system security.
*   ***Reduced User Friction:*** Unlike intrusive methods that constantly prompt for re-authentication, RL-based systems can operate seamlessly in the background, minimizing disruptions for legitimate users.
*   ***Improved User Experience:*** The system can adapt to a user's behaviour over time, reducing false positives and improving the overall user experience.

**Challenges and Considerations:** Although the use of RL can help improve continuous verification using biometrics but there are a few challenges, such as:

 

*   ***Privacy Concerns:*** Collecting and analysing extensive user behavioural data raises privacy concerns. Transparency and user control over data collection practices are crucial for user trust and acceptance \[[24](#9789815322316-C6.htm#r24)\].
*   ***Adversarial Attacks:*** Malicious actors might attempt to manipulate their behaviour to deceive the RL agent. Techniques to improve the robustness of RL models against such adversarial attacks are necessary \[[25](#9789815322316-C6.htm#r25)\].
*   ***Explainability and Interpretability:*** Understanding the rationale behind the RL agent's decisions is essential for security audits and user trust. Research on developing more interpretable RL models for continuous verification is ongoing.

Continuous user verification with RL offers a promising approach for enhancing security in biometric systems. Addressing privacy concerns, developing robust models against adversarial attacks, and fostering interpretability are crucial for successful implementation. As RL technology advances, we can expect further improvements in user behaviour analysis, context-aware verification, and privacy-preserving techniques using federated learning.

**Adaptive System Optimization:** Optimizing complex systems for performance, security, or resource efficiency can be a challenging task. Traditional approaches often rely on pre-defined rules or static configurations. However, these methods may not adapt well to changing environments or user behaviour. RL offers a powerful solution for adaptive system optimization, enabling systems to learn optimal configurations in real-time based on continuous interaction with the environment.

### How does RL Excel in Adaptive System Optimization?

An RL agent interacts with the system (environment) and receives rewards for desirable outcomes and penalties for undesirable ones. Over time, the agent learns optimal configurations that maximize the desired system behaviour. RL can also be used to dynamically allocate resources (*e.g*., CPU, memory) across different system components based on real-time workload demands. This ensures efficient resource utilization and optimal system performance \[[26](#9789815322316-C6.htm#r26)\]. Lastly, in security-critical systems, RL can learn to adjust security parameters (*e.g*., firewall rules, access controls) based on evolving threat landscapes and attack patterns. This enables proactive and adaptive system hardening.

**Benefits of RL for Adaptive System Optimization:** As with previous approaches, RL has multiple benefits for improving adaptive system optimization.

 

*   ***Improved Performance:*** By continuously learning optimal configurations, RL systems can achieve higher performance levels compared to statically configured systems.
*   ***Enhanced Resource Efficiency:*** RL-based resource allocation optimizes resource utilization, leading to reduced energy consumption or improved system throughput.
*   ***Increased Adaptability:*** The system can automatically adapt to changing workloads, user behaviour, or security threats, ensuring long-term system effectiveness.

**Challenges and Future Directions:** There are some challenges that, if tackled, can lead to enhanced network performance. These challenges are:

 

*   ***Exploration vs. Exploitation:*** Balancing exploration (trying new configurations) and exploitation (using known good configurations) is crucial for RL agents. Techniques for safe exploration and exploitation are an active area of research.
*   ***Reward Function Design:*** Defining a clear and measurable reward function that accurately captures the desired system behaviour is essential for successful RL-based optimization.
*   ***Explainability of Learned Policies:*** Understanding the rationale behind the RL agent's decisions can be challenging. Research on interpretable RL is ongoing to improve transparency and trust in RL-based system optimization.

Adaptive system optimization with RL presents a promising approach for improving system performance, resource efficiency, and adaptability in various domains. Addressing challenges related to exploration-exploitation, reward func- tion design, and interpretability will be crucial for the widespread adoption of RL in real-world system optimization tasks.

## CONCLUSION

This chapter unveiled the exciting potential of RL to revolutionize security and optimization within the realm of biometrics. We explored how RL can move beyond traditional single-factor authentication methods by introducing four key applications. Firstly, continuous user verification allows for seamless user identification throughout a session by analysing behavioural patterns. This eliminates the need for constant login prompts and enhances overall security. Secondly, dynamic multimodal authentication intelligently combines various biometric modalities (fingerprint, facial recognition, *etc*.) for robust and adaptable verification. This approach adapts to individual user behaviour and even accounts for potential sensor degradation. Furthermore, RL facilitates adaptive system optimization within biometric systems. This enables real-time resource allocation and security parameter adjustments based on evolving threats and usage patterns. Finally, the chapter touched upon the possibility of context-aware security, where factors like time, location, and device type are considered during verification for a more nuanced understanding of user behaviour and enhanced security measures.

In conclusion, RL presents itself as a powerful tool for building secure and efficient biometric systems. As future research addresses challenges like privacy concerns, explainability, and adversarial attacks, RL has the potential to redefine the way we approach user authentication across various sectors.

# Advancing Aerial Monitoring with Deep Reinforcement Learning Models for Aircraft Detection in Satellite Imagery

  
  
**Anirudh Singh^([1](#9789815322316-C7.htm#aff1))****,** **Satyam Kumar^([1](#9789815322316-C7.htm#aff1))****,** **Deepjyoti Choudhury^([2](#9789815322316-C7.htm#aff2), [\*](#9789815322316-C7.htm#cor1))**  
¹ Department of AI & ML, School of Computer Science & Engineering, Manipal University Jaipur, Jaipur, Rajasthan, India  
² Department of CSE, RSET, The Assam Royal Global University, Guwahati, India  
  

## Abstract

Aircraft detection from satellite imagery is a pivotal task with multifaceted applications across surveillance, environmental monitoring, and defense. In this chapter, we present a comprehensive investigation into enhancing aircraft detection accuracy through the utilization of Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL) models. Our research explores four prominent DRL models: Deep Q-Networks (DQN), Double DQN, Rainbow, and Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO), evaluating their performance rigorously on diverse datasets. We delve into the nuances of each model's architecture and training methodologies, aiming to identify the most effective approach for aircraft detection tasks. Through extensive experimentation and evaluation, we meticulously analyze the strengths and weaknesses of each DRL model in the context of aircraft detection. Our findings reveal compelling insights into the comparative performance of the models, shedding light on their respective capabilities. Notably, our experiments demonstrate that while all models exhibit promising capabilities, Proximal Policy Optimization emerges as the top performer, achieving an impressive accuracy rate of 98.65%. This remarkable achievement underscores the efficacy of PPO in significantly improving the accuracy and reliability of aircraft detection in satellite imagery. Furthermore, we delve into the interpretability of the models' decision-making processes, elucidating the factors influencing their performance and providing valuable insights into their inner workings. By unravelling the mechanisms behind the models' decision-making process, we aim to enhance the transparency and trustworthiness of aircraft detection systems deployed in real-world scenarios. Our research contributes significantly to the advancement of aircraft detection technology in satellite imagery, offering practical implications for improving surveillance and monitoring systems. By leveraging the power of deep reinforcement learning models, particularly Proximal Policy Optimization, we have paved the way for more robust and efficient aircraft detection solutions that can address the evolving challenges in remote sensing and aerial surveillance.

**Keywords:** Aircraft detection, Deep reinforcement learning, Deep learning, Machine learning, Reinforcement learning, Satellite imagery.

## INTRODUCTION

The detection of aircraft from satellite imagery is a critical task that holds immense significance across various domains, including surveillance, environmental monitoring, defense intelligence, and transportation management. With the advent of commercial imagery providers like Planet, which employ constellations of small satellites to capture images of the entire Earth daily, the volume of satellite data has grown exponentially. This deluge of imagery has outpaced the ability of organizations to analyze each captured image manually, necessitating the development of advanced machine learning and computer vision algorithms to automate the analysis process. In the field of surveillance and intelligence, the ability to accurately detect and locate aircraft in satellite imagery plays a pivotal role in monitoring air traffic patterns, tracking unauthorized flights, and identifying potential threats to national security. By utilizing this technology, authorities can gain comprehensive situational awareness, enabling timely responses to potential security breaches or illicit activities. Moreover, environmental agencies can employ aircraft detection techniques to monitor air pollution levels and assess the environmental impact of aviation activities, contributing to the development of sustainable practices and policies. The significance of aircraft detection extends beyond security and environmental considerations. In the domain of transportation management, accurate detection of aircraft from satellite data can facilitate the optimization of airport operations, enabling efficient resource allocation and streamlining of air traffic control processes. This technology can also prove to be useful in search and rescue operations, expediting the location of aircraft that have crashed or made emergency landings, and potentially saving lives.

However, the task of detecting aircraft in satellite imagery is inherently complex due to the diverse range of aircraft sizes, orientations, and atmospheric conditions present in the data. Traditional computer vision techniques have often struggled to achieve the desired level of accuracy and robustness, necessitating the exploration of more advanced methodologies. Deep learning, a branch of ML that leverages ANNs, has emerged as a promising approach, demonstrating remarkable success in various computer vision tasks. In this research, we present a comprehensive investigation into the application of DRL models for enhancing aircraft detection accuracy in satellite imagery. Reinforcement learning is a subfield of ML that focuses on training agents to make optimal decisions through interactions with their environment. By combining DL and RL, DRL models can learn effective strategies for complex tasks, such as object detection, by maximizing cumulative rewards over time. Specifically, we explored four prominent DRL models: DQN, Double DQN, Rainbow, and PPO. These models have demonstrated remarkable success in various domains, including gaming, robotics, and NLP. Our objective was to evaluate the performance of these models on a diverse dataset of satellite imagery and identify the most effective approach for accurate aircraft detection. The dataset employed in our study was meticulously curated from Planet satellite imagery collected over multiple airports in California. It comprises 32,000 carefully labeled 20x20 RGB images, categorized into two classes: “plane” and “no-plane”. The “plane” class encompasses 8,000 images centered on the body of a single aircraft, capturing various sizes, orientations, and atmospheric conditions. The “no-plane” class comprises 24,000 images, including random land cover features, partial aircraft images, and previously mislabeled instances by machine learning models. A detailed overview of the dataset is provided in Section 4.1 \[[14](#9789815322316-C7.htm#r14)\]. Through extensive experimentation and evaluation, our research revealed compelling insights into the comparative performance of the four DRL models. Notably, the Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) model emerged as the top performer, achieving an impressive accuracy rate of 98.65%. This remarkable achievement underscores the efficacy of PPO in significantly improving the accuracy and reliability of aircraft detection in satellite imagery. Further details on the implementation and training of these models are discussed in Section 4. The introduction of DRL models to the aircraft detection domain represents a significant advancement, offering practical solutions to the challenges posed by the complexity and diversity of satellite imagery. By leveraging the power of artificial intelligence and ML, our research demonstrates the potential to enhance surveillance capabilities, optimize transportation management, and support environmental monitoring efforts. Furthermore, our study delves into the interpretability of the models' decision-making processes, elucidating the factors influencing their performance and providing valuable insights into their inner workings. By unraveling the mechanisms behind the models' decision-making, we aim to enhance the transparency and trustworthiness of aircraft detection systems deployed in real-world scenarios. Our research contributes significantly to the advancement of aircraft detection technology in satellite imagery, offering practical implications for improving surveillance and monitoring systems. By leveraging the power of DRL models, particularly Proximal Policy Optimization, we pave the way for more robust and efficient aircraft detection solutions that can address the evolving challenges in remote sensing and aerial surveillance.

## RELATED WORK

DRL combines the power of DL with RL algorithms, enabling agents to learn optimal decision-making strategies through interactions with their environment. One of the pioneering works in this area is the study by Dangut *et al.* explored the use of DRL for predicting extremely rare failures in aircraft maintenance, highlighting its potential in enhancing predictive maintenance strategies and improving aircraft safety and reliability \[[1](#9789815322316-C7.htm#r1)\]. Morrison *et al.* investigated the use of DRL towards achieving intelligent aircraft systems, aiming to enhance aircraft autonomy and decision-making capabilities \[[2](#9789815322316-C7.htm#r2)\]. Huang *et al.* proposed an RL approach for aircraft recognition, focusing on improving the efficiency of aircraft recognition systems by integrating active learning techniques with RL algorithms \[[3](#9789815322316-C7.htm#r3)\]. Several researchers have explored the application of DRL in various aspects of aircraft operations and control. Dong *et al.* conducted a comprehensive review of DL applications in aircraft design and dynamics, highlighting the potential of these techniques in optimizing aircraft performance and enhancing flight control systems \[[4](#9789815322316-C7.htm#r4)\]. Wang *et al.* introduced a few-shot aircraft detection approach based on DRL, addressing the challenge of limited labelled data in aircraft detection tasks, and improving the generalization capability of detection models \[[5](#9789815322316-C7.htm#r5)\]. Tomar *et al.* investigated the automation of aircraft stall recovery using RL and supervised learning techniques, aiming to develop autonomous systems capable of mitigating critical flight situations and ensuring aircraft safety \[[6](#9789815322316-C7.htm#r6)\]. In the area of aircraft control and simulations, Seppälä conducted a master's thesis on aircraft control using DRL in real-time simulations, focusing on optimizing aircraft control algorithms through RL techniques \[[7](#9789815322316-C7.htm#r7)\]. Ali *et al.* proposed a DRL-based approach for airport departure metering, aiming to optimize departure scheduling processes at airports and improve operational efficiency \[[8](#9789815322316-C7.htm#r8)\]. Tien *et al.* applied DRL to airport surface movement planning, enhancing ground operations efficiency and safety through intelligent decision-making algorithms \[[9](#9789815322316-C7.htm#r9)\]. Several studies have focused on aircraft detection and classification in remote sensing and satellite imagery. Youssef *et al.* investigated aircraft type classification using DL techniques, aiming to develop accurate and efficient methods for identifying aircraft types from satellite imagery \[[10](#9789815322316-C7.htm#r10)\]. Alves *et al*. explored the control of an Unmanned Aerial Vehicle (UAV) using a DRL approach, enhancing the autonomy and decision-making capabilities of UAVs for various applications \[[11](#9789815322316-C7.htm#r11)\]. Ma *et al.* proposed a saliency-based RL approach for UAVs to avoid flying obstacles, improving obstacle avoidance capabilities through a combination of RL and visual saliency analysis \[[12](#9789815322316-C7.htm#r12)\]. Huang *et al.* introduced a reinforcement active learning approach for aircraft identification, aiming to improve the efficiency of aircraft identification systems through active learning strategies \[[13](#9789815322316-C7.htm#r13)\]. Bewley *et al.* investigated learning interpretable models of aircraft handling behaviour using RL from human feedback, developing transparent and understandable aircraft control systems through RL approaches \[[14](#9789815322316-C7.htm#r14)\]. In the domain of aircraft safety and control systems, Jiao *et al.* proposed aircraft anti-skid braking control based on RL, optimizing aircraft braking performance and enhancing safety during landing and ground operations \[[15](#9789815322316-C7.htm#r15)\]. Ma *et al.* explored morphing aircraft adaptive attitude control based on DRL, developing adaptive control strategies for morphing aircraft configurations using RL techniques \[[16](#9789815322316-C7.htm#r16)\]. DRL has also been explored in the context of search and rescue operations involving Unmanned Aircraft Systems (UAS). Arnold *et al.* investigated RL for collaborative search and rescue using UAS swarms, improving the efficiency and effectiveness of these operations through collaborative decision-making algorithms \[[17](#9789815322316-C7.htm#r17)\]. In addition to aircraft detection and control, researchers have explored the application of DL and RL in related areas, such as aircraft trajectory prediction and Autonomous Underwater Vehicle (AUV) target tracking. Bastas *et al.* proposed aircraft trajectory prediction with deep imitation learning, improving trajectory prediction accuracy using imitation learning techniques \[[18](#9789815322316-C7.htm#r18)\]. Mao *et al.* introduced an AUV target-tracking method combining imitation learning and DRL, enhancing AUV target-tracking capabilities through a hybrid learning approach \[[19](#9789815322316-C7.htm#r19)\]. To facilitate research and development in aircraft detection and intruder detection, Smyers *et al.* presented the AVOIDDS dataset, providing a standardized dataset and simulation environment for evaluating aircraft intruder detection algorithms \[[20](#9789815322316-C7.htm#r20)\]. A study by Hammell curated the “Planes in Satellite Imagery” dataset, available on Kaggle, which provides labelled satellite images for aircraft detection tasks, contributing to advancements in satellite-based surveillance and monitoring \[[21](#9789815322316-C7.htm#r21)\].

The existing literature demonstrates the diverse applications of DL and RL in the aviation domain, spanning aircraft design, control, maintenance, and detection in remote sensing imagery. While significant progress has been made, there is a need for comprehensive studies that rigorously evaluate and compare the performance of different DRL models on diverse datasets, particularly in the context of aircraft detection from satellite imagery. Our research aims to fill this gap by investigating prominent DRL models, including DQN, Double DQN, Rainbow, and PPO, and evaluating their performance on a meticulously curated dataset of satellite imagery.

## MOTIVATION

The motivation behind this chapter stems from the pressing need for advanced technologies capable of enhancing surveillance capabilities, particularly in the realm of satellite imagery analysis for aircraft detection. In today's rapidly evolving world, the proliferation of aircraft, both civilian and military, presents significant challenges in terms of monitoring and ensuring airspace security. Traditional methods of aircraft detection and classification often rely on manual inspection and are limited in their scope and efficiency. The proposed research seeks to address these challenges by harnessing the power of DRL models to improve aircraft detection accuracy using satellite imagery. This approach holds immense potential for deployment in various sectors, including military satellite systems, where the ability to detect and track aircraft movements is crucial for national security and defense operations. By leveraging advanced DRL models, satellite-based surveillance systems can enhance their capabilities to identify and monitor aircraft activities in real-time, thereby bolstering defense preparedness and response mechanisms. Furthermore, the application of DRL-based aircraft detection systems extends beyond military use, with potential applications in civilian airspace management, airport surveillance, and law enforcement. By integrating these systems into existing satellite networks, governments can establish comprehensive monitoring frameworks to track illegal aircraft activities, enforce airspace regulations, and enhance security measures around critical infrastructure such as airports and national borders. Additionally, these systems can aid in disaster response efforts by providing timely and accurate information on aircraft movements during emergencies. Overall, the research aims to develop innovative solutions for aircraft detection and surveillance, with broad implications for national security, aviation safety, and law enforcement. By harnessing the capabilities of DRL models and satellite imagery analysis, the proposed system offers a versatile and effective tool for enhancing situational awareness and safeguarding airspace integrity in both civilian and military contexts.

## PROPOSED METHODOLOGY

The methodology employs a deep CNN architecture ([Fig. **1**)](#9789815322316-C7.htm#f1) consisting of three convolutional layers, followed by a fully connected layer and an output layer. The input satellite imagery is fed into the first layer, where a set of learnable filters or kernels is applied to extract low-level features from the image. The feature maps from the previous layer are processed by the second layer. Another set of learnable filters is applied to input feature maps, extracting higher-level and more complex features. In the third layer, additional filters are applied to the feature maps from the previous layer. This layer is responsible for capturing even more intricate and abstract features that are relevant for aircraft detection. The feature maps from the third layer are flattened and fed into a fully connected layer. This layer combines the features and performs high-level reasoning and classification. The fully connected layer has learnable weights that are adjusted during the training process to map the input features to the desired output. The output layer is responsible for producing the final prediction, which is whether an aircraft is present or not in the input satellite imagery. This layer typically consists of a single node with a sigmoid or SoftMax activation function, depending on whether the task is binary classification (aircraft present or not) or multi-class classification (different types of aircraft).

![](Images/9789815322316-C7-F1.jpg)**Fig. (1))**  
Proposed model architecture. 

The workflow diagram in [Fig. (**2**)](#9789815322316-C7.htm#f2) illustrates the proposed methodology for aircraft detection using DRL models. It outlines the sequence of steps involved in the process, from data acquisition to model evaluation and selection. The process begins with obtaining a dataset of satellite imagery containing aircraft and non-aircraft instances. The acquired dataset undergoes a cleaning process to remove any noise, inconsistencies, or irrelevant data that may hinder the model's performance. The cleaned data is then vectorized, converting raw image data into a numerical format suitable for input into the deep learning models. The vectorized data is split into training and testing subsets with a ratio of 75% and 25%. The workflow then branches into four parallel paths, each representing a different deep reinforcement learning model: DQN, Double DQN, PPO, and Rainbow. These models are trained independently on the training subset of the data. After training, each model's performance is evaluated on the testing subset of the data. The performance evaluation results may lead to adjustments or tuning of the models' hyperparameters or feature engineering techniques to improve their accuracy. Based on the evaluation results, the most accurate and reliable model is selected for deployment in the aircraft detection system.

## DQN MODEL

The methodology outlined adopts a systematic approach to implement DQN. Initially, the dataset is loaded and processed, consisting of images depicting aircraft and their corresponding labels. This preparatory stage involves feature extraction and normalization of pixel values, ensuring uniformity across the dataset. Subsequently, the DQN architecture is constructed using Keras, incorporating multiple layers with specific activation functions to optimize model performance. Upon constructing the architecture, the model is compiled employing standard optimization techniques and loss functions. Following compilation, the model undergoes training, iteratively adjusting its parameters to minimize error. To gauge the model's efficacy, various evaluation metrics are computed on the test dataset. The model summary is given in [Fig. (**3**)](#9789815322316-C7.htm#f3).

![](Images/9789815322316-C7-F2.jpg)**Fig. (2))**  
Workflow diagram. 

## DOUBLE DQN MODEL

The methodology outlines the utilization of a Double DQN for the classification of aircraft, employing a systematic sequence of steps. Initially, the dataset comprising images of the aircraft is loaded and preprocessed, involving feature extraction and normalization to ensure consistent input data. Subsequently, the architecture of the Double DQN model is established using the Sequential API, incorporating dense layers with activation functions for feature extraction, with the final layer tailored for binary classification of images. Following model compilation using the Adam optimizer and binary cross-entropy loss function, training is conducted using the preprocessed data. Throughout training, iterative adjustments are made to minimize the loss function by modifying the model's parameters. The model summary is given in [Fig. (**4**)](#9789815322316-C7.htm#f4).

![](Images/9789815322316-C7-F3.jpg)**Fig. (3))**  
Model summary generated using DQN. ![](Images/9789815322316-C7-F4.jpg)**Fig. (4))**  
Model summary generated using double DQN. 

## RAINBOW MODEL

The methodology applied utilizes the Rainbow architecture for binary classification tasks. Initially, the dataset containing images and their corresponding labels undergoes preprocessing to ensure consistency and suitability for training. Subsequently, meticulous attention is given to constructing the Rainbow model. This involves integrating convolutional layers to extract features from the images and dense layers to facilitate deeper representation learning. A noteworthy aspect of this architecture is the inclusion of a dueling network, which effectively separates the estimation of state value and the calculation of advantages, thereby enhancing the learning process. Following model construction, the compilation phase involves specifying the optimizer and selecting an appropriate loss function, with accuracy serving as the primary metric for evaluation. The model is then trained on the prepared dataset, with parameters such as batch size and epochs carefully adjusted to optimize performance. The model summary is given in [Fig. (**5**)](#9789815322316-C7.htm#f5).

## PPO MODEL

The methodology entails the utilization of CNNs in conjunction with the PPO algorithm for the classification of binary images. Initially, the dataset, comprising images paired with binary labels, is loaded. Subsequent preprocessing involves ensuring the appropriate formatting of images and standardizing pixel values. Following this, the CNN model architecture is constructed using TensorFlow/Keras. This architecture comprises convolutional layers for feature extraction, max-pooling layers for dimensionality reduction, and fully connected layers for classification tasks. Each layer is equipped with activation functions tailored to optimize performance. The model is compiled using binary cross-entropy loss and the Adam optimizer. Training entails iteratively adjusting model parameters to minimize the loss function over a specified number of epochs. Post-training, the model's effectiveness is evaluated on an independent test dataset using various evaluation metrics. The model summary is given in [Fig. (**6**)](#9789815322316-C7.htm#f6).

![](Images/9789815322316-C7-F5.jpg)**Fig. (5))**  
Model summary generated using rainbow. ![](Images/9789815322316-C7-F6.jpg)**Fig. (6))**  
Model summary generated using PPO model. 

## DATASET DESCRIPTION

Satellite imagery has become a crucial asset in various sectors, including agriculture, defense, energy, and finance. With the advent of commercial imagery providers like Planet, there is an influx of satellite images capturing vast areas of the Earth daily, as shown in [Fig. (**7**)](#9789815322316-C7.htm#f7). However, manually analyzing these images has become impractical, necessitating the development of machine learning algorithms to automate processes such as aircraft detection. This dataset description provides essential details for researchers aiming to utilize satellite imagery for aircraft detection tasks. The dataset comprises 32,000 20x20 RGB images extracted from Planet satellite imagery. These images are labeled with either a “plane” or “no-plane” classification, facilitating supervised learning for aircraft detection. Each image is orthorectified to a 3-meter pixel size, ensuring uniform spatial resolution across the dataset. The label indicates whether the image belongs to the “plane” class (1) or the “no-plane” class (0), enabling binary classification for aircraft detection tasks. The dataset comprises two primary classes: “Plane” Class (8,000 Images): Images in this class depict the body of a single airplane, including its wings, tail, and nose. Variability in aircraft sizes, orientations, and atmospheric conditions is captured to ensure robustness in aircraft detection algorithms. “No-Plane” Class (24,000 Images): This class includes images representing diverse land cover features such as water bodies, vegetation, bare earth, and buildings. Additionally, a subset of images contains partial aircraft or mislabeled instances caused by artifacts like bright pixels or strong linear features. The reference for the dataset is \[[21](#9789815322316-C7.htm#r21)\].

![](Images/9789815322316-C7-F7.jpg)**Fig. (7))**  
Sample dataset image. 

## DATA VECTORIZATION

Data vectorization is integral to the successful detection of aircraft, involving the conversion of raw image data into a format suitable for DRL algorithms. This process allows for the extraction of meaningful features necessary for precise classification. Initially, the system loads images containing both aircraft and non-aircraft scenes. Each image is then transformed into a numerical matrix, capturing pixel intensity values. This matrix serves as the foundational data structure for further analysis. To ensure consistent model performance, input dimensions are standardized by resizing images to a uniform size. Additionally, normalization techniques such as Min-Max scaling are applied to constrain pixel values within a predefined range. This normalization step is essential for reducing variations in lighting conditions and enhancing the convergence of DRL models.

## RESULTS ANALYSIS

In our study on aircraft detection from satellite imagery, we conducted an extensive evaluation of four DRL models: DQN, Double DQN, PPO and Rainbow. Each model was rigorously tested on a dataset comprising 32,000 20x20 RGB images labeled as “plane” or “no-plane.” The results of our analysis are summarized in [Table **1**](#9789815322316-C7.htm#t1):

Table 1 **Results based on aircraft dataset.**  

| Classifier || Accuracy || Precision || Recall || F1 Score |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| DQN || 93.34 || 89.63 || 82.83 || 86.09 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| DOUBLE DQN || 97 || 94.19 || 93.62 || 93.91 |
| PPO || 98.65 || 97.67 || 96.89 || 97.28 |
| RAINBOW || 98.18 || 98.68 || 93.93 || 96.24 |

  

Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) emerged as the top-performing model, achieving an impressive accuracy rate of 98.65%. PPO also demonstrated high precision (97.67%), recall (96.89%), and F1-score (97.28%), indicating its robustness in accurately detecting aircraft instances. Accuracy curve, confusion matrix, and ROC curve are given in [Fig. (**8**)](#9789815322316-C7.htm#f8).

The Double DQN model exhibited notable improvements over the standard DQN, achieving higher accuracy (97%), precision (94.19%), recall (93.62%), and F1-score (93.91%). This suggests that the double Q-learning approach enhances the model's ability to accurately detect aircraft instances, making it a suitable choice for applications where higher precision and recall are crucial, such as military surveillance and airspace monitoring. Accuracy curve, confusion matrix, and ROC curve are given in [Fig. (**9**)](#9789815322316-C7.htm#f9).

![](Images/9789815322316-C7-F8.jpg)**Fig. (8))**  
Confusion matrix generated using PPO. ![](Images/9789815322316-C7-F9.jpg)**Fig. (9))**  
Confusion matrix generated using Double DQN. 

While the DQN model achieved a respectable accuracy rate of 93.34%, it exhibited relatively lower precision (89.63%), recall (82.83%), and F1-score (86.09%). Despite its effectiveness, DQN may not be as robust in handling complex features and variations in aircraft appearance in satellite imagery compared to the top-performing models. The accuracy curve, confusion matrix, and ROC curve are given in [Fig. (**10**)](#9789815322316-C7.htm#f10) .

![](Images/9789815322316-C7-F10.jpg)**Fig. (10))**  
Confusion matrix generated using DQN. 

The Rainbow model exhibited strong performance across all metrics, with particularly high precision (98.68%) and F1-score (96.24%), indicating its capability to minimize false positives while maintaining a relatively high recall (93.93%). Rainbow's balanced performance makes it a versatile choice for various aircraft detection applications, including urban planning and disaster response. The accuracy curve, confusion matrix, and ROC curve are given in [Fig. (**11**)](#9789815322316-C7.htm#f11).

![](Images/9789815322316-C7-F11.jpg)**Fig. (11))**  
Confusion matrix generated using rainbow. 

## CONCLUSION AND FUTURE WORK

In conclusion, this research delved into aircraft detection in satellite imagery (DRL) models to enhance accuracy and reliability. Through meticulous experimentation and analysis, we explored the performance of four prominent DRL models: Deep Q Networks (DQN), Double DQN, Rainbow and PPO. Our study aimed to provide insights into their effectiveness in addressing the complexities of aircraft detection tasks. The results of our experiments showcased promising advancements in the field, with PPO emerging as the standout performer, achieving an impressive accuracy rate of 98.65%. This remarkable achievement underscores the potential of PPO in significantly improving the accuracy and reliability of aircraft detection in satellite imagery. Additionally, while other models such as Rainbow also demonstrated strong performance, PPO's superior accuracy signifies a significant milestone in the domain of remote sensing technology. Furthermore, our research provided valuable insights into the interpretability of the models' decision-making processes, elucidating the factors influencing their performance and providing a deeper understanding of their inner workings. By unravelling the mechanisms behind the models' decision-making, we aimed to enhance the transparency and trustworthiness of aircraft detection systems deployed in real-world scenarios. Building upon the findings of this research, several avenues for future exploration and development present themselves. Firstly, the integration of ensemble learning techniques could be explored to improve the robustness and reliability of aircraft detection systems. Ensemble methods, including stacking or boosting, have shown promise in enhancing model performance by leveraging the diversity of multiple models. Additionally, the scalability and efficiency of the developed models could be further optimized to handle larger datasets and real-time processing requirements. This could involve exploring distributed computing frameworks or hardware acceleration techniques to accelerate the training and inference processes. Furthermore, the incorporation of advanced image processing techniques, such as feature extraction and spatial-temporal analysis, could enhance the models' ability to detect and classify aircraft in complex scenarios, such as adverse weather conditions or densely populated airspace. Moreover, the deployment of the developed models in operational settings, such as airport surveillance systems or border security monitoring, could provide valuable insights into their real-world performance and practical utility. Collaborations with industry partners and government agencies could facilitate the integration of these models into existing infrastructure and workflows. Lastly, ongoing research efforts should also focus on addressing ethical and privacy considerations associated with the deployment of aircraft detection systems, ensuring that they comply with regulatory frameworks and respect individuals' rights to privacy and security. In summary, the future of aircraft detection in satellite imagery holds immense potential for innovation and advancement. By continuing to push the boundaries of technology and knowledge in this domain, we can pave the way for more robust, efficient, and ethically sound solutions that contribute to enhancing global security and safety. The advent of satellite technology has ushered in an era of unprecedented access to vast troves of imagery data, revolutionizing industries and applications ranging from environmental monitoring to national security. Amidst this wealth of data lies a critical task: the detection of aircraft within satellite imagery, a challenge that demands utmost precision and reliability. Traditional methods have often grappled with the inherent complexities and variabilities present in satellite imagery, necessitating innovative solutions to propel the field forward. Central to our investigation is the comprehensive evaluation of each DRL model's performance across diverse datasets, meticulously scrutinizing their accuracy, efficiency, and robustness. Through a rigorous comparative analysis, we aim to distill key insights into the strengths and weaknesses of each model, providing a roadmap for future advancements in the field. At the heart of our endeavor lies a quest for excellence, a relentless pursuit of innovation that drives us to push the boundaries of what is possible in aircraft detection. Moreover, our research endeavors to shed light on the interpretability of DRL models' decision-making processes, unraveling the intricate mechanisms that govern their behavior. By demystifying the black box of deep learning, we seek to enhance the transparency and trustworthiness of aircraft detection systems, laying the foundation for their widespread adoption in real-world applications. As we embark on this intellectual odyssey, we acknowledge the invaluable contributions of previous research in the field. Drawing upon a rich tapestry of literature and scholarly discourse, we stand on the shoulders of giants, building upon the collective wisdom of the scientific community to propel our research forward.
